The Comprehensive Development Plan (CDP) is a multi-sectoral development output of the City of Santa Rosa in the Province of Laguna. The plan sets the development agenda and direction of the city for the next six (6) years – 2023-2028. The CDP, from its inception to its completion, was rigorously crafted through the participation of various stakeholders from all levels, such as Civil Society Organizations (CSOs), Non-Government Organizations (NGOs), private organization (POs) and National and Local Government Agencies and Offices. The formulation of the CDP is guided by the guidelines from the Department of Interior and Local Government (DILG).

The plan is divided into four (4) major chapters: LGU Profile, Matrix of Local Indicators, Sectoral Development Plan and Local Development and Investment Plan (LDIP).

The LGU Profile covers the history, population, demography and geo-physical characteristics of the city. It also provides information on the different sectors – social, economic, environment, institutional and infrastructure that will make up the sectoral development plan of the CDP.

Matrix of Local Indicators is a combination of development indicators from the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and the DILG’s Local Governance Performance Management System (LGPMS) that are relevant to the needs of the city. Data from these indicators are used to monitor and assess the state of development in the City of Santa Rosa.

Sectoral Development Plan is a collection of action plans from each sector that is guided by the vision, mission, goals and objectives of the City of Santa Rosa and aligned to the Philippine Development Plan 2023-2028, the Sustainable Development Goals, the Ambisyon 2040 and the Regional Development Plans. Each plan aims to address all the issues and concerns in each sector resulting to programs, projects and activities that will achieve the goals and objectives set by each sector for the improvement of the city’s services.

The Local Development Investment Plan serves as the primary implementing tool of the CDP which includes the Ranked List of PPAs, Project Briefs and the use of budgetary instruments and resource management strategies in developing a financing plan that duly considers the investible portion of the local budget given its fiscal management capability. Monitoring and Evaluation Strategies, outlined and presented in this chapter, will be used to ensure the efficient, timely and effective implementation of PPAs that will complete the cyclical nature of planning.

![image 55](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile55.png>)

Santa Rosa’s historical past can be traced back to 1571. During that time, Santa Rosa was called Barrio Bukol and was a part of Biñan, a barrio to the town of Tabuco (Cabuyao at present). Later in 1688, Biñan together with Barrio Bukol was separated from Cabuyao. Barrio Bukol, in turn, was politically emancipated as the Municipality of Santa Rosa on January 18, 1792. The town was named after Saint Rose of Lima of Peru.

During the revolutionary period, Santa Rosa was instrumental in the proclamation of Philippine Independence from Spain when it signed the Act of Independence on June 12, 1898. Later on, the town’s local revolutionaries fought alongside the forces of General Pio del Pilar during the short lived Filipino-American War. After being briefly occupied by the Japanese during World War II, Santa Rosa was liberated by members of the Filipino guerilla resistance movement on February 5, 1945.

During the postwar era until the 1970’s, the people of the municipality were still largely dependent on basic agriculture and family-owned enterprises for livelihood. The influence of industrialization slowly took shape in the 1980’s with the entry of local and foreign investors who were instrumental in the rapid economic and social transformation of the town.

From a fourth class municipality with an average income of P4 million in 1986, Santa Rosa became a first class town when its income reached P54.2 million in 1993. In 1998, the municipality’s annual revenue further increased due to intensified tax collection. Then became a City of Santa Rosa on the 10th of July 2004 by virtue of Republic Act No. 9264 dated March 10, 2005. The City together with Biñan City and San Pedro City constitutes the First District of Laguna. It was on August 28, 2019 when President Rodrigo Duterte signed Republic Act No. 11395 that separated the city from the First District and constituted the Lone district of the City of Santa Rosa.

Currently, the City of Santa Rosa is the premier city and hub of South Luzon and has been recognized as a fast rising investment capital of the region next to Metro Manila.

The City of Santa Rosa is situated in the western section of Laguna de Bay, nearly 40 kilometers south of Manila. It is accessible via the South Luzon Expressway through 3 exits: Santa Rosa Exit (39km); Greenfield City-Eton City-ABI Exit (42 km) and Greenfield City/UNILAB (Mamplasan Biñan Exit) (36 km) to SM City Santa Rosa via Biñan-Santa Rosa Road. Its proximity and accessibility from Metro Manila makes it a strategic and ideal place for local and foreign businesses as well as people looking for a suburban residence near the capital city (CLUDP 2000 – 2015).

Geographically, it is located at 121ᴼ 5’ 48° latitude and 14° 16’ 43° longitude. It is bounded on the north by Biñan City , on the south by Cabuyao City and on the west by the Province of Cavite. With a total land area of 5,543 hectares (55.43 square kilometers), the city comprises 2.89 percent of the total land area of the Province of Laguna.

###### FIGURE 1.1. Geographical Location Map, City of Santa Rosa

![image 60](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile60.png>)

SOURCE: GIS Division, City Planning and Development Office

City of Santa Rosa has 18 barangays. Three of these barangays are located at the coastal Laguna Lake namely Barangay Aplaya, Barangay Sinalhan and Barangay Caingin. Twelve barangays are at low land area and these are barangays Balibago, Dila, Dita, Malitlit, Macabling, Malusak, Pooc, Market Area, Ibaba, Labas, Kanluran and Tagapo. The remaining three barangays: Barangay Santo Domingo, Don Jose and Pulong Santa Cruz are situated at the upper side going to Tagaytay City.

The largest barangay is Barangay Don Jose accounting to 19.07 % of the City’s total land area, followed by barangays Malitlit and Santo Domingo with 18.78% and 14.53% respectively. The smallest barangay is Barangay Malusak comprising less than one fourth of Santa Rosa’s total land area.

###### FIGURE 1.2. Barangay Area Map, City of Santa Rosa

![image 63](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile63.png>)

SOURCE: GIS Division, City Planning and Development Office

The City of Santa Rosa is generally flat with certain areas of rolling terrain. There are two types of terrain in the city: Level to very gently sloping terrain and undulating to rolling terrain. Level to very gently sloping terrain are areas with slopes ranging from 0.0 to 2.5% and are ideal for any sort of development. Undulating to rolling terrain, on the other hand, are areas characterized by slopes of 5.0 to 18.0% and are susceptible to erosion.

###### FIGURE 1.3. City Contour Map, City of Santa Rosa

![image 66](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile66.png>)

SOURCE: GIS Division and GEODATA, City Planning and Development Office

The city’s soil type is categorized into five (5) main types: Carmona Clay Loam, Lipa Loam, Guadalupe Clay, Carmona Sandy Clay Loam, and Quingua Fine Sandy Lo3am. The City’s soil type is favorable for farming of crops which could provide livelihood for city residents and could provide a steady supply of fresh produce to the market.

###### TABLE 1.1. Soil Types, Characteristics and Location, City of Santa Rosa, Laguna

![image 69](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile69.png>)

SOURCE: CLUP 2020 – 2015, LCCAP 2016-2025 ,City of Santa Rosa

###### FIGURE 1.4. Soil Type Map, City of Santa Rosa

SOURCE: GIS Division, City Planning and Development Office

![image 70](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile70.png>)

Despite having multiple soil types, the City is mainly categorized to only two (2) types of rocks namely, Clastic Rock and Alluvium Rock. Alluviums are loose, unconsolidated soil or sediments which were eroded and reshaped by water in some form, and redeposited in a non-marine setting. On the other hand, its soil of clastic rocks provides high quality potable water for residential, commercial and industrial uses. These good water bearing characteristics makes the town an ideal site for major investment areas by the private sectors.

###### FIGURE 1.5. Rock Type Map, City of Santa Rosa

SOURCE: GIS Division, City Planning and Development Office

![image 73](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile73.png>)

###### GEO-PHYSICAL HAZARDS

Earthquake Hazard. The region of Laguna is strongly faulted given the presence of the Marikina Valley Fault which traverses a north-south direction. The fault is disrupted by a number of minor steep faults along its course that are reflective of the volcanic activity relative to the area. Earthquakes are a hazard in Santa Rosa as the West Marikina Valley Fault traverses Sto. Domingo.

###### FIGURE 1.6. West Valley Fault Line Map, City of Santa Rosa

![image 76](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile76.png>)

SOURCE: PHILVOCS(Data) and GIS Division(Map), City Planning and Development Office

Slope/Soil Erosion. The City of Santa Rosa is almost entirely on a plain with 97.89% of its total land area having slopes ranging from 0.0 to 2.5% indicating level to nearly level lands. Only about 114 hectares or 2.06% of the total land area of the city is subject to slight erosion. The area most susceptible to erosion is the south-western tip of Barangay Santo Domingo.

###### FIGURE 1.7. Slope Map, City of Santa Rosa

![image 79](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile79.png>)

SOURCE: GIS Division, City Planning and Development Office

###### FIGURE 1.8. City Waterways Map, City of Santa Rosa

![image 80](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile80.png>)

The City is principally drained by Santa Rosa River, Diezmo River, and Cabuyao River which all flow from the mountainous area of Silang, Cavite towards Laguna Lake. Serving as natural boundaries between Santa Rosa and Cabuyao are the Diezmo River and Cabuyao River. These rivers drain out to Laguna de Bay, the largest lake in the Philippines.

In terms of risk of liquefaction, however, the City‘s geological and soil status poses a low-tohigh risk of liquefaction with high risk areas identified to lake shore barangays.

SOURCE: GIS Division, City Planning and Development Office

The current climate of Santa Rosa is characterized as Type 1 under the Modified Coronas Classification of Philippine Climate. This type has two pronounced seasons: dry from December to April and wet from June to October. May and November are seasonal transition months from dry to wet season and vice versa.

Santa Rosa has an annual mean temperature of 27.4°C with temperatures peaking around May at more than 29 ºC and cooling down in January at nearly 25 ºC. Observed baselines for rainfall are 629.2 mm for December to February, 386.8 mm for March to May, 845 mm for June to August, and 1,066.5 mm from September to November. Typhoon season starts in May reaching up to November and sometimes up to December.

There are no studies which present the local climate trends for Santa Rosa, but data at the national level indicates that for the past six decades, the annual mean temperature in the country increased by 0.57°C. The number of hot days was reported by PAGASA (2011) to increase while the number of cold nights decreased. The number of typhoons remains the same but there was a slight increase in the maximum sustained winds for the number of typhoons that were recorded. (LCCAP 2016-2025)

###### PROJECTED CLIMATE

The climate projections for the City of Santa Rosa, Laguna were based on the projected changes of different climate variables for 2020 and 2050 using the observed baseline from 1971 to 2000 (PAGASA, 2011). The climate projection information includes temperature, rainfall, and frequency of extreme events.

TABLE 1.2. Projected Changes in Climate Variables in the Mid-21st Century (2036-2065)

for Laguna relative to 1971-2000

|Climate Variable|Observed Baseline (1971-2000)|Specific Change Expected and Reference Period (Increase)|General Changes Expected in Climate Variables|Information about Patterns of Change|Exposure Unit|
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|Temperature|25.0 ℃ (DJF)|25.9 ℃ by 2020,<br><br>26.8 ℃ by 2050 (DJF)<br><br><br>|Increasing temperature for all seasons expected in 2020 & 2050<br><br>|Temperature increase in all months|All|
| |27.5 ℃ (MAM)|28.6 ℃ by 2020,<br><br>29.6 ℃ by 2050 (MAM)<br><br><br>| | | |

|Rainfall<br><br>|629 mm (DJF)<br><br>|502 mm by 2020, 630 mm by 2050 (DJF)<br><br>|Decreasing in rainfall by 2020 and very slight increase by 2050|Reduction in rainfall during Amihan months in DJF and summer in 2020 and 2050. Increase in rainfall during Habagat (JJA) and SON of 2020 & 2050<br><br>|All|
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| |387 mm (MAM)|265 mm by 2020, 252 mm by 2050 (MAM)<br><br>|Decreasing in rainfall by 2020 and 2050| | |
| |845 mm (JJA)|870 mm by 2020, 903 mm by 2050 (JJA)<br><br>|Increasing in rainfall by 2020 and 2050| | |
| |1067 mm (SON)|1097 mm by 2020, 1071 mm by 2050 (SON)<br><br>|Increasing in rainfall by 2020 and 2050| | |
|Number of hot days (>35 ℃)|928 days|8010 days exceeding 35 ℃ in 2020<br><br>8016 days exceeding 35 ℃ in 2050<br><br>|Increasing number of fish holidays (exceeding 35 ℃)|Significant increase in the number of hot days expected in 2020 and 2050|All|
|Number of dry days|8226 days|6081 days with <2.5 mm of rain in 2020<br><br>6049 days with <2.5 mm of rain in 2050<br><br>|Decreasing number of dry days (<2.5 mm of rain)|There will be more days with rainfall (less days with dry days compared to baseline)|All|

Data Source: Climate Risk Analysis for Laguna, Philippine Atmospheric, Geophysical and Astronomical Services Administration (PAGASA) Notes: 2050 is the middle of the period between 2036 and 2065; Seasons: DJF is December-January-February; MAM is March, April and May; JJA is June, July and August; SON is September, October and November.

Temperature. Laguna will experience an increase in temperature in 2020 and 2050 for all seasons. There are observed small variations in average temperature across the seasons, with less warm temperatures during the DJF(December, January, February) months, and warmer temperatures from March to August. Based on the projected climate for the mid-range scenario at the provincial level of Laguna (indicative for the city of Santa Rosa), temperatures are projected to increase up to 1.1°C in 2020 and up to 2.2 °C in 2050.

Rainfall. Historically, Laguna receives the least rainfall during the months of MAM (March,April,May) and the highest rainfall amount during the months of SON (September,October,November). This is based on the average rainfall data for 30 years from 1971 to 2000. By 2050, the seasonal patterns of rainfall distribution will still be the same with the months of MAM receiving the least and the months of SON receiving the most rainfall. However, the amount will change with a 13.80 % increase in rainfall during DJF and an 11% reduction during the months of JJA (June,July,August).

Extreme events. The number of hot days is expected to have a significant increase by 2020 and will continue by 2050. At the same time, there will be a decrease in the number of dry days in 2020 and 2050, as compared to the baseline. With this, it is expected to have an increase in the extreme daily rainfall of >200 mm for 2020 and 2050.

The frequency of extreme rainfall events (days with rainfall exceeding 300mm) will increase to 14 days in 2006 - 2035 and nine days in 2036 – 2065, from the observed frequency of six days in 1971-2000. The frequency of dry days will decrease from 8,226 in 1971 to 2000 to 6,081 in 2006 to 2035 and 6,049 in 2036 to 2065. The frequency of extreme temperature events (days with maximum temperature exceeding 35°C) will increase to 8,010 in 2006 to 2035 and 8,016 in 2036 to 2065, from the observed frequency of 928 in 1971 to 2000.

These projections point to much hotter and drier summers for Santa Rosa with more days with extreme temperatures over 35 °C. While the projected increase in average rainfall during the rainy season is not as large as the projected decrease in the summer season, there will be more days with extreme rainfall over 300mm (LCCAP 2016-2025).

###### CLIMATE CHANGE IMPACT FOR THE CITY OF SANTA ROSA

Based on the projected patterns of change in the climate considering the identified climate variables, Table 1.3. shows the possible impact areas or sectors in the City of Santa Rosa.

Negative effects on the City’s ecosystem and the economy were found to be most concerning about the changing climate. With an increased temperature above the observed baseline, the environment sector expects a higher risk of forest fire and drought throughout the year. The phenomenon of drought affects Santa Rosa’s water supply while worsening the quality of the soil. Higher temperature also reduces the growth rate of trees and may well affect the overall health of the population. Meanwhile, an increased number of hot days means growth in demand for water, both for domestic and agricultural purposes. Intensifying competition among sectors and a rise in demand for water increases production costs which potentially affects agricultural production and normal functions of businesses and households. In lakes, warmer days pave way for algal bloom, producing fish with a foul odor.

###### TABLE 1.3. Climate Change Impact for the City of Santa Rosa, Laguna

![image 89](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile89.png>)

SOURCE: Outputs of CDRA Workshop organized by DILG Santa Rosa

A decrease in the amount of rainfall during the Amihan season is crucial to the agriculture sector. Drought, pest multiplication, and reduced recharging rate of the water reservoir shape unfavorable conditions for food production. The said factors will affect the food supply of Santa Rosa. In addition, this is also crucial to the households and commercial establishments as drought, vermin, and weather-borne diseases, and depletion of freshwater aquifers are unfavorable to them as it may cause an increase in the cost of water.

Extreme events such as excessive rainfall were found to cause an overflow of clogged drainages. Although the flooding is below one meter and usually subsides within an hour, the normal flow of networks, services, and businesses might be disrupted. This has adverse effects on the City’s economy. Excessive rainfall associated with frequent tropical cyclones may induce soil erosion and landslides which leads to damage on, properties, livelihoods, and tourism amenities.

Figure 1.9. illustrates the data presented in Table 1.3. on the impacts of climate change in the city.

![image 92](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile92.png>)

CLIMATE-RELATED HAZARDS AND IMPACTS

Hazards are categorized as natural and anthropogenic. Geophysical hazards such as earthquakes, erosion and volcanic eruptions as well as climate and weather-related hazards are natural hazards. It is a natural process or event that is potentially damaging and may result in loss of life or property, disruption of systems, socio-economic destruction, and environmental degradation. Anthropogenic hazards, on the other hand, are hazards caused by human action or inaction.

Flooding. Natural flooding caused by an overflow of the drainage system and high tide combined with the City’s topography affects coastal barangays and low lying areas. During typhoons with strong rains, almost all areas in the City have pockets of flooding.

On the other hand, the population at high risk exposure to floods is about 30,531 or 11% of the total population of the city. ###### All barangays in the City of Santa Rosa are vulnerable to ground shaking and 83.3% are prone to flooding. Barangays from Cluster I together with Barangays Dila, Dita and Pooc in Cluster II are at risk of liquefaction due to their geological and soil status. Only Barangay Sto Domingo is vulnerable to landslide both rain- and earthquake -induced and at risk of ground rupture since it is located along the West Marikina Valley Fault Line.

###### FIGURE 1.12. River Rain Induce Landslide Map, City of Santa Rosa, Laguna

SOURCE: GIS Division, City Planning andDevelopment Office

Several areas in the city are susceptible to liquefaction. According to the US Geological Survey (USGS), “liquefaction takes place when loosely packed, water-logged sediments at or near the ground surface lose their strength in response to strong ground shaking.” This can cause major damage in buildings and other structures during earthquakes. Brgys. Sinalhan, Aplaya, Market Area, Kanluran, Ibaba, Malusak and Caingin are highly susceptible to liquefaction. Brgys. Tagapo and Labas have moderate susceptibility to liquefaction, while Pooc has moderate to high susceptibility. Areas of low susceptibility to liquefaction include Brgys. Macabling, Balibago, Dila and Dita. (CLUP 2018-2026)

City of Santa Rosa is a 1st class component city in the province of Laguna. Out of the 108 component cities in the Philippines, the city ranked 8 on the country’s ten most populous component cities of 2020. (Source: 2020 PSA)

The population of the City of Santa Rosa consistently increased but with a fluctuating trend in its growth rate as shown in Figure 1.14. Based on the 2020 Census of Population and Housing (CPH), the City of Santa Rosa has a total population of 414,812 accounting for 12.26% of Laguna province total population and 2.67% of the CALABARZON population.

This record is higher than the provincial and regional growth rate of 2.30 and 2. 48, respectively. Population Density. One of the indicators of population distribution is the population density which is expressed as the number of persons per unit of land area usually in hectares or square kilometer. With a population of 414, 812, and a land area of 55.43 square kilometers, the 2020 population density of Santa Rosa City is 7,483 persons per square kilometers.

From Table 1.8., the population density of the city is directly proportional to its population size and inversely proportional to its population growth rate. The continuous increase in the number of birth due to population momentum and the emerging status of the city as location of middle to high-class subdivision and condominiums will further contribute to the increase in population despite of the decreasing trend in its growth rate.

City of Santa Rosa is divided into 18 barangays: Aplaya, Balibago, Caingin, Dila, Dita, Don Jose, Ibaba, Kanluran, Labas, Macabling, Malitlit, Malusak, Market Area, Pook, Pulong Santa Cruz, Santo Domingo, Sinalhan and Tagapo. Brgy. Malitlit has the largest share in land area with 10.28 square kilometers. followed closely by Brgy. Don Jose with 10.26 square kilometers land share. These two barangays, together with Barangays Pulong Santa Cruz (4. 46 sq. km.) and Dita (4.27 sq. km.) comprises more than half of the city’s land area. On the other hand, Brgy. Malusak is the smallest barangay with a land area of 0.15 square kilometers. Other barangays with land area that is less than 1 square kilometers are: Brgy. Kanluran (0.19 sq. km.), Brgy. Market Area (0.43 sq.km.), Brgy. Ibaba (0.44 sq. km.) Brgy. Aplaya (0.72 sq. km.) and Brgy. Labas (0.90 sq. km.).

The most populous barangay is Brgy. Pooc with 55,705 residents accounting to 13.34 percent of the city’s population. The next most populated barangays are: Brgy. Tagapo (10.15%), Brgy. Dila (9.52%), Brgy. Dita(8.35%) and Brgy. Pulong Santa Cruz (7.09%). It was in 2015 when Brgy Tagapo raised its rank from 5th most populated barangay with 7.22% share in the 2010 population to second most populated barangay with 9.52% share in the 2015 population. A 2.3% change in Brgy. Tagapo’s population was due to the establishment of private subdivisions around Amihan Village and Tiongco Subdivision. This continues as new subdivisions are established near SM Santa Rosa.

The least populated barangay is Brgy. Malusak, with a 0.70 percent share in the 2020 population of Santa Rosa. Other less populated barangays are Brgy. Kanluran (1.02%), Brgy. Santo Domingo (1.65%) and Brgy. Ibaba (1.95%).

Barangay Santo Domingo posted the highest growth rate of 11.50 percent while Barangay Kanluran recorded a negative population change of – 2.53 percent, together with Brgy. Don Jose and Brgy. Ibaba with – 0.95 percent and – 0. 57 percent change, respectively.

The most densely populated barangay is Barangay Market Area with 37,336 persons per square kilometers while the least densely populated is Barangay Sto. Domingo with only 823 persons per square kilometers.

Urbanization. Another indicator of population distribution is the extent of urbanization. PSA defines an urban area as: (1) a barangay that has a population size of 5,000 or more; or (2) a barangay has at least one establishment with a minimum of 100 employees; or (3) a barangay has 5 or more establishments with a minimum of 10 employees and 5 or more facilities within the two-kilometer radius from the barangay hall (NSCB, 2003).

In terms of population size, the majority of the barangays (89%) have a population that is greater than 5,000. Only two barangays (11%) have less than 5,000 population count namely: Brgy. Malusak and Brgy. Kanluran. However, these two barangays are strategically located at the heart of Santa Rosa City where many fast food chains, 24/7 convenience stores, commercial, retail and financial establishments are situated, satisfying the other requirements of an urban area.

With the population data shown on the above table and the number of establishments with the required number of employees as set forth by PSA in its definition of an urban area, all the barangays in the City of Santa Rosa are 100 percent urban.

Household Population. The Philippine Statistics Authority (PSA) defines a household as a social unit consisting of a person living alone or a group of persons who sleep in the same housing unit and have a common arrangement in the preparation and consumption of food. Household population refers to all persons who are members of the household.

The institutional population constitutes that part of the total population whose usual place of residence are institutional living quarters (ILQs) such as hotels, lodging houses and dormitories; hospitals and nurses’ homes; orphanages and welfare institutions; corrective and penal institutions; convents, nunneries, seminaries, and boarding schools; military camps; logging, mining, construction and public works camps; oceangoing, interisland, and coastal vessels; and refugee camps.

TABLE 1.10. Institutional and Household Population, Number of Households, Average Household Size of City of Santa Rosa, Year 2010, 2015, 2020

![image 124](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile124.png>)

SOURCE: PSA Population Census 2020

Based on the 2020 Census of Population and Housing (CPH), the household population of the City of Santa Rosa comprised 99.7 percent of the city’s total population while the remaining 0.3 percent accounts to the institutional population. With a population of 414,812 residents, there are 122,458 households in Santa Rosa City and an average household size of 3.4 as shown on Table 1.10. The 20.79 percent increase in the number of households in 2020 is only half the percent increase of the number of households in 2015.

On the other hand, the institutional population in 2020 is 1, 173 which is six times more than the recorded institutional population in 2015. The 998 difference in the number of institutional population was due to the growing number of establishments and manufacturing companies in the upper side of Santa Rosa going to Tagaytay, the presence of the BPO industry in Brgy Tagapo and the COVID-19 pandemic.

TABLE 1.11. 2020 CPH Historical and Household Population Projection Per Barangay, City of Santa Rosa

![image 125](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile125.png>)

SOURCE: 2020 PSA Population Census

Table 1.11. shows the result of the 2020 CPH Barangay Population for the City of Santa Rosa as per Proclamation No. 1179. With the given average household size of 3.40, the number of households was determined and the 2020 population and household data for each barangay were projected up to Fiscal Year 2029. The population used in this table includes the individuals residing in various institutional living quarters. By 2023 and 2029, the projected number of households in the City of Santa Rosa is 135,145 and 165, 828, respectively.

Population composition is the description of the characteristics of a group of people in terms of factors such as their age, sex, marital status, and socio-economic status such as education, occupation, language and religion. These factors are used to analyze and predict social and economic trends and determine social services needed to address the current and future socio-economic situations. Of these, the age and sex composition of any population are most widely used. Graphically, population composition is represented using a population pyramid or the age-sex pyramid. This diagram shows the size and influence of a city’s population of different ages between men and women.

The table below shows the age-sex distribution of the household population of the City of Santa Rosa for census year 2015 and 2020.

###### TABLE 1.12. Household Population Age-Sex Distribution by 5-Year Age Group, City of Santa Rosa, 2015 & 2020

![image 128](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile128.png>)

SOURCE: 2015 and 2020 Philippine Statistics Authority Population Census

Based on the 2020 Census of Population and Housing (2020 CPH), the City of Santa Rosa had a total population of 414,812 persons. Of this total, the household population comprised 99.7 percent or 413,639 persons.

![image 131](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile131.png>)

Of the 413,639 household population in 2020, 205, 522 (49.69%) were males while 208,117 (50.31%) were females. By age group, 110,269 (26.7%) were under 15 years of age (young dependents). On the other hand, persons aged 15 to 64 years (workingage or economically-active population) totaled 287,986 (69.6%) while those in age groups 65 years and over (old dependents) comprised the remaining 15,384 (3.7%). In 2015, persons aged 0 to 14 years, 15 to 64 years, and 65 years and over accounted for 25.9, 71.1, and 3 percent, respectively, of the household population.

###### FIGURE 1.15. Sex Composition, City of Santa Rosa, 2020 CPH

Moreover, there were more females (66.7%) than males (64.1%) among the 20 and above age group. Meanwhile, among the younger age group (under 20 years), males (35.9%) outnumbered the females (33.3%). The same trend was also observed in 2015.

###### Population Pyramid of the City of Santa Rosa

Figure 1.16. is the graphical representation of the Age-Sex distribution of the household population in Table 1.12. From the figure, the male and female household population in 2020 is more evenly distributed compared to the male and female household population in 2015. By age group, the working-age population comprises the most number of households in both years although there is a minimal increase in the household population of young and old dependents in 2020.

FIGURE 1.16. Population Pyramid of City of Santa Rosa: 2020 and 2015

![image 132](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile132.png>)

###### TABLE 1.13. Number and Percent to All Ages of Household Population and Sex Ratio by Selected Age Group: City of Santa Rosa, 2020 and 2015

![image 135](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile135.png>)

SOURCE: Philippine Statistics Authority, 2020 Census of Population and Housing and 2015 Census of Population

Sex Ratio. The ratio between the number of men and women in the population is known as the sex ratio It is usually expressed as the number of males for every 100 females. Sex ratio may vary due to different patterns of death and migration for males and females within the population.

The sex ratio in the City of Santa Rosa was computed at 99 in 2020. This means that there were 99 males for every 100 females. The sex ratio in 2015 was slightly lower at 97 males per 100 females. (Figure 1.17.)

In 2020, children aged below 15 years had a sex ratio of 108 males per 100 females, while those aged 15 to 64 years had a sex ratio of 98 males per 100 females. Moreover, among those aged 65 years and over, the sex ratio was 66 males per 100 females. This depicts a longer life expectancy among females than males or a higher mortality rate among males than females in the older age groups. (Figure 1.17.)

FIGURE 1.17. Sex Ratio by Selected Age Group, City of Santa Rosa, 2020

![image 137](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile137.png>)

SOURCE: PSA Population Census 2020

Children below five years old comprised 9.1 percent (37,706) of the household population which is slightly higher than the 8.9 percent (31,488) posted in 2015. The sex ratio of 109 males per 100 females in 2020 is higher than the recorded sex ratio (140 males per 100 females) in 2015. (Table 1.13.)

Median Age. The median age of the City Santa Rosa population continues to rise during the past three census years. In 2020, the median age was calculated at 27.4 years, which means that half of the household population was younger than 27.4 years, while the other half was older than 27.4 years. This is higher than the median ages of 26.6 years and 25 years that were posted in 2015 and 2010, respectively.

FIGURE 1.18. Median Age of Household Population, City of Santa Rosa: 2020, 2015 and 2010

![image 139](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile139.png>)

SOURCES: Philippine Statistics Authority, 2010 and 2020 Census of Population and 2015 Census of Population

Dependency Ratio. Dependency ratio shows the number of dependents to the population. A low dependency ratio is desirable because it indicates that there are proportionally more adults of working age who can support the young and elderly of the population. It signifies a better social services for the dependents are provided.

The overall dependency ratio of the City of Santa Rosa was computed at 44, which indicates that for every 100 working-age or economically-active population, there were about 44 dependents (38 young dependents and 6 old dependents). In other words, one dependent is supported by two economically-active individuals. This is higher than the dependency ratio in 2015, which was recorded at 41 dependents per 100 working-age population (37 young dependents and 4 old dependents). (Table 1.14.)

TABLE 1.14. Age Dependency Ratio of the Household Population by Sex: City of Santa Rosa, 2020 and 2015

SOURCES: Philippine Statistics Authority, 2010 and 2020 Census of Population and 2015 Census of Population NOTE: Details may not add up to total due to rounding.

School-Age Population. In 2020, the school-age population (5 to 24 years old) accounted for 147,643 (35.7%) of the 413,639 household population, which is lower compared to the 131,946 (37.3%) school-age population that was reported in 2015. (Table 1.14. and Figure 1.19)

Individuals belonging to this age group are considered of voting age for the Sangguniang Kabataan in accordance with Republic Act No. 8044 (Sangguniang Kabataan Act of 2015). (Table 1.13.)

Voting-Age Population. The voting-age population (18 years old and over) accounted for 68.7 percent (284,312) of the household population in 2020, a slight decrease from 68.9 percent (243,727) in 2015. (Table 1.13. and Figure 1.20)

Aging index. The Aging Index refers to the number of elderly people (aged 60 years and over) per 100 individuals younger than 15 years old in a specific population. Thus, the higher the index is, the older the population results. In the City of Santa Rosa, the aging index was computed at 24.2 percent in 2020. This means that there is one person aged 60 years and over for every four children under 15 years old. In 2015, the aging index was 3.7 points lower at 20.5. (Table 1.13. and Figure 1.23.)

FIGURE 1.23. Aging Index, City of Santa Rosa: 2020 and 2015

SOURCE: Philippine Statistics Authority, 2020 Census of Population and Housing

Ethnicity. Almost everyone in Santa Rosa City is Filipino of various ethnic origins. The original settlers in Santa Rosa are Tagalogs. Throughout the centuries, there are constant migration of Spaniards, Visayans, Bikolanos, Ilokanos, Chinese and Americans. At present, the top five major ethnic groups in the city are Tagalogs, Visayans, Caviteños, Bikolanos and Ilocanos respectively.

Religion. Most residents of Santa Rosa are Roman Catholics including Catholic Charismatics. Other groups represented include Iglesia Ni Cristo, Aglipayans (members of Iglesia Filipina Independiente which is a breakout group from the Catholic Church in 1902 by the Union Obrera Democratica), Jesus Is Lord Church, Evangelicals, Baptist, Church of Christ, Jehovah's Witness, Seventh Day Adventists, Protestants and Islam.

Languages/dialects. The main language is Filipino, which is based on Tagalog. In addition, due to continuous development of the city, English is used in education, business and information technology. Other dialects include Bisaya, Bikolano, Ilokano, Pangasinense, Kapampangan and Caviteño.

Poverty Incidence. Poverty incidence, as defined by PSA, is "the proportion of poor individuals whose per capita income is not sufficient to meet their basic food and non-food needs." City of Santa Rosa had the highest poverty incidence in 2015 at 2.4 and the lowest at 1.29 in 2018 which is also the lowest poverty incidence rate in the whole province of Laguna that year. Figure 1.24 shows the poverty incidence rate of the city from years 2006, 2009, 2012, 2015 and 2018.

###### FIGURE 1.24. Poverty Incidence, City of Santa Rosa: 2006,2009,2012,2015 and 2018

SOURCE: Philippine Statistics Authority

###### City of Santa Rosa provided education from pre-school to secondary and offers various courses including technological and vocational courses through its tertiary schools and manpower training centers. Below is a table that shows the number of schools for each level of education.

###### TABLE 1.15. Number of Schools Per Level: City of Santa Rosa, SY 2020-2021 and SY 2021-2022

![image 155](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile155.png>)

SOURCE: City Planning and Development Office

There are one hundred eleven (111) schools in the city consisting of eighteen (18) public schools and eightythree (83) private schools that provide education services from various levels. Ninety (90) of these schools (18 public, 72 – private) offer education from pre-school to elementary. Secondary education provided in the city are offered by forty-eight (48) schools for Junior High school and thirty-four (34) schools for Senior High School, majority of which are from private institutions. The city also provided education for learners with special needs in both elementary and secondary. In SY 2020-2021, there were seven (7) public and private schools that offer special education to learners in elementary and one (1) private school that offer Special Education to high school learners. At present, only learners with special needs in elementary level had enrolled and received special education from three public schools (Balibago Elementary School, Dita Elementary School and SRES Central III) and four private schools (Holy Rosary College of Sta Rosa Laguna Inc, Mt. Rushmore Academy of Laguna, Shepherd of Faith SPED Center and Work of Heart Child Center).

There are eighty-four thousand, four hundred forty nine (84,449) learners enrolled in school year 2021-2022 from elementary to senior high school education from both public and private institutions. This number of enrollees comprises 90% of the school-age population (elementary to high school) of the city, which shows an increase in the participation rate from 89% of SY 2020-2021 to 93% participation rate in SY 2021-2022.

There are nine (9) tertiary schools in the city: eight (8) are private and one (1) is public. The Polytechnic University of the Philippines -Santa Rosa is the sole public tertiary school and offers 12 Bachelor of Science courses. In AY 2021-2022, almost 44% (2,552) of the 7,959 enrolled tertiary learners were from PUP. The remaining 56% (3,142) comprises the enrollees in the private colleges and universities. Out of the 7,959, 64.17%, that is 5,107 learners, graduated from various courses on arts, science and technology. In the tertiary level, the teacher-to-student ratio (TCR) was 1:27 while the classroom-to-student ratio (CSR) was 1:44 which were higher compared to the ratios of the previous academic year.

###### TABLE 1.16. Tertiary School and Training Center Indicators, City of Santa Rosa, SY 2020-2021 and SY 2021-2022

![image 158](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile158.png>)

SOURCE: CityPlanningand DevelopmentOffice

Technical and vocational courses are provided through Santa Rosa Manpower Training Center, Alternative Learning Schools (ALS) and six (6) private training centers in the city. There were 963 enrollees from the two public training centers and 1,447 enrollees from the private training schools for AY 2021-2022. From this pool of learners, 1,957 graduates of different technical and vocational courses accounts to the academic year’s 81.20% graduation rate which is lower than the 99.96% graduation rate in AY 2020-2021.

Health services in the City of Santa Rosa are provided by two (2) city health offices, twenty-four (24) barangay health stations, nine (9) hospitals, one hundred twenty-two (122) clinics and thirty-two (32) laboratories and diagnostic centers for the year 2022.

The two City Health Offices (CHO I and II) covers all eighteen (18) barangays, each with nine (9) barangays under their care. CHO I provided services to Barangay Aplaya, Caingin, Ibaba, Kanluran, Labas, Malusak, Market Area, Sinalhan and Tagapo while CHO II covers Barangays Balibago, Dila, Dita, Don Jose, Macabling, Malitlit, Pook, Pulong Sta. Cruz, and Santo Domingo. Services offered by both CHO may differ but includes the different clusters of health programs, namely: Family Cluster that covers maternal and infant health care programs, Noncommunicable diseases programs that include but not limited to Diabetes and other Endocrine Programs, Cardiovascular Control and Prevention Program, and Communicable Diseases Programs like TB Control and Prevention Program, Rabies Control and Prevention Program and other Emerging and Re-emerging Infectious Diseases which includes the COVID-19 Pandemic. (LIPH 2019-2022)

Seven (7) barangays have two (2) Barangay Health Stations (BHS) each, to cater to their larger population. Thus, the city has a total of twenty four (24) BHS: eleven (11) BHS from CHO I and thirteen (13) BHS from CHO II. There are also plans of having strategic areas for the establishment of birthing facilities as well as a super health center. This will further improve the health services provided to the people and in accordance with the implementation of Universal Health Care. (Santa Rosa City Health Office,2022)

The nine (9) hospitals in the city are composed of one (1) public hospital and eight (8) private hospitals with services under level II to III category. Details on these hospitals including their accreditation and capacity for emergency services are seen in the table below.

There is an improvement in the health service capacity of the city in 2022 as expressed in the personnel-topopulation ratio in Table 1.18. This is due to the increase in the number of doctors, nurses and medical technologists despite of a slight decrease in the number of midwives.

Santa Rosa Community Hospital is the only public hospital in the city. Housing is one of the basic necessities in every family. As per Section 16 (General Welfare) of the local government code, every local government unit, within their respective territorial jurisdictions, shall preserve the comfort and convenience of their inhabitants. One of which is to provide its inhabitants a good housing settlement that is convenient and will make them feel safe and protected.

Housing condition in the City of Santa Rosa is generally satisfactory as needs for dwelling units are provided by one hundred seventy-five (175) residential subdivisions that are distributed to 18 barangays.

Barangay Tagapo has the most number of subdivisions whose classification ranges from second to sixth class while Barangay Kanluran has only one subdivision under the third class type. More than half of the subdivision are under the RS3 and RS4 classification and are situated on barangays around the city proper. On the other hand, first class subdivisions are built on the three barangays at the upper side going to Tagaytay City. These are Barangay Don Jose, Barangay Malitlit and Barangay Santo Domingo. In Barangay Santo Domingo, all the eleven (11) subdivisions are first class residential property.

On turned over properties as illustrated in Figure 1.26, Barangay Tagapo has the most number of turned over subdivisions. Amihan and Tiongco subdivision are among these turned over property which provides access to the various government facilities like the Santa Rosa Multi-Purpose Sports Complex, Slaughterhouse, Women and Children Crisis Center and Santa Rosa Drug Rehabilitation Center

###### Informal Settlements

One of the major problems in housing is the presence of informal settlers (ISFs) in the community. City of Santa Rosa like other local government units has informal settlers in its fifteen (15) barangays. No presence of ISFs in Barangay Don Jose, Barangay Malusak and Barangay Kanluran as seen in Table 1.23. Among the 15 barangays with ISFs, Barangay Pulong Santa Cruz has the largest ISFs population with 2,123 households followed by Barangay Macabling with 1,172 households.

###### TABLE 1.23. Housing Units for Informal Settlers per Barangay, City of Santa Rosa: Year 2022

![image 196](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile196.png>)

SOURCE: CityUrbanDevelopmentandHousing Office

It was in 2017 when the number of ISFs household in the city diminished from 7,946 ISFs households to 6,170 ISFs households. However, eighty (80) ISFs households were added to this population in 2021. This 1.3% increase in the number of ISFs households occurred during the time when the massive effect of COVID-19 was experienced in the city. At present, there are six thousand two hundred fifty (6,250) ISFs households associated to fifty one (51) communities in the informal settlement area and six thousand seven hundred twenty-five (6,725) relocatees distributed to thirteen relocation sites in the resettlement area.

There are five thousand eight hundred thirty (5,830) dwelling units in the informal settlement area distributed to 15 barangays and ten thousand two hundred fifty-seven (10,257) housing units in the resettlement area occupied by the 6, 725 relocatees from the city and the 3,532 remaining recipients of the housing units. The housing units in the resettlement area are built on thirteen (13) relocations sites acquired through a Community Mortgage Program and provided by the National Housing Authority and the City Government of Santa Rosa.

There are forty-five (45) day care centers in the 18 barangays that are fully functional with complete facilities in good condition. Each center is manned by a Day Care Worker which provides substitute mothering for the 3-4 years old. In 2021, the total number of children from these centers were 1,258 resulting to a day care workerchildren ratio of 1:47 compared to the standard of 1:30 ratio. To comply with the standard, some day care workers provide two or more sessions in a day. The number of sessions in a day care center depends on the number of its registered children.

The general economic activity of the city is described by the actual land use that took place as seen in Table

- 1.21. Land use implications of this became pronounced as more agricultural areas gave way to industries, commercial and residences.

With the favorable opening of the South Luzon Expressway (SLEX) in the 1980s, at least 21 globally competitive locator companies, mainly in manufacturing and industry, invested in the town. First to come in was the Filipinas Synthetic Fiber Corporation (Filsyn) in Barangay Don Jose, Ani Forge Philippines Inc. and Coca Cola-Bottling Company Inc. in Barangay Pulong Sta.Cruz, and Monde Nissin in Barangay Balibago. There were also 725 commercial enterprises established in the city. Investors and businesses came from the wholesale and retail sector, banking and finance sector, insurance and real estate, and services. (LED Plan 2021-2025)

At present, Santa Rosa City has one hundred seventy five (175) subdivisions; Seven (7) major malls SM City; Robinson’s, Vista Mall, Nuvali (Solenad Malls), Paseo de Sta. Rosa Mall, Target Mall and Victory Central Mall; forty three (43) multi-national factories, one hundred twenty-two (122) banks, two hundred eight (208) ATMs, one hundred twenty two (122) pawnshop/lending investors, money changers/foreign exchange and remittances; four (4) commercial complexes and one (1) theme park. It has nine (9) vital installations, MERALCO sub-stations; and two (2) telephone companies namely: PLDT and Globe Telecoms. The city’s small and medium scale enterprises are engaged in the areas of metal craft, furniture making, business retailing, personal and

business services, food enterprises and communal lake fishing and farming. There are three (3) Industrial Parks straddling the cities of Santa Rosa and Biñan, namely: Laguna Technopark, Inc., Santa Rosa Business Park and Meridian Industrial Park Complex. The rapid industrialization of the city is best represented by an array of premiere commercial businesses and Industrial estates which significantly transformed its old rural landscape to three (3) major estates currently progressing and accelerating the city’s development.

Majority of business activities in 2021 are from the secondary and tertiary business sectors, particularly the trading and construction industry. The business activity with the highest average growth rate in five years, inclusive from 2017 to 2021, is from the Real Estate industry. The increase in the number of investors in the City of Santa Rosa from these industries was due to the city’s proximity to Metro Manila. Although, the effect of COVID-19 pandemic in the city is evident through the decrease in the business activities in 2019 and 2020, the confidence of business industries to the city’s economic capability and resiliency is visible in the succeeding year.

###### MAJOR CIRCULATION NETWORK

Transport systems, by the mobility they provide, are closely related to socioeconomic changes. Economic opportunities will likely arise where transportation infrastructures can ensure access to markets and resources. (Jean-Paul Rodriguez, 2020, p. 90). Thus, the city government provided transport support to the business sector by allowing the private sectors to establish various terminals into the city. The mode of transportation around the city includes tricycles, buses, public utility jeepneys and vans. Pedicabs have its route around villages and subdivisions. In terms of accessibility, the city has a wide access on buses going to Cubao and LRT Buendia and to provinces like Bicol, Iloilo and Leyte ; public utility vans going to Alabang, Cubao, LRT Buendia, Lemery via Tagaytay, Lipa City, Dasmariñas Cavite, San Pablo City, Sta. Cruz, Laguna and Lucena City, Batangas; public utility jeepneys going to Biñan City, San Pedro City, Cabuyao City, Calamba City and Tanauan, Batangas. In 2022, there are three (3) bus terminals, fourteen (14) van terminals, seven (7) jeepney terminals and one thousand two hundred (1,200) tricycle terminals. The city has a total of 41 Tricycle Operators and Drivers’ Associations (TODA) to date.

###### SOURCES OF WATER

There are two (2) surface water resources in the City of Santa Rosa: the Santa Rosa River and Laguna Lake. Santa Rosa River flows from the watersheds of Silang, Cavite towards Laguna de Bay. The river is under the class C category according to DENR and is suitable for aquaculture, fisheries, recreation and extraction for industrial uses. Easements of 3-4 meters from both sides throughout the entire length of the river under class C are considered environmentally critical and are subject to easement for public use.

Laguna Lake, the largest lake in the Philippines has a total surface area of 90,000 hectares, an average depth of

- 2.8 meters, and a total volume of 3.2 billion cubic meters. The lake water is only suitable for aquaculture, fisheries, recreational activities and industrial uses. Although the lake is naturally euphoric and highly productive, it is polluted as a result of human activities in the watershed carried via tributaries or directly into the lake.

Groundwater resources are accessible due to the good water bearing capabilities of the alluvium and clastic rocks underlain in Santa Rosa. Thus, areas in Pulong Sta. Cruz, Malitlit, Don Jose and Sto. Domingo can be utilize for potentially high yielding wells to access groundwater. However, saltwater intrusion can happen when waters from Manila Bay flush into the lake and lakeshore communities exceed the safe yield limits of their aquifers.

From the wellhead assessment project conducted by the city in cooperation with Coca Cola Foundation and Wildlife Fund for Nature (WWF)-Philippines in 2009, a total of one thousand eight hundred sixty-six (1,866) communal wells are identified in 18 barangays. Among these wells, about 1,104 have been used for drinking purposes by around 8,465 families. Majority of these wells are located in barangays Aplaya, Caingin and Sinalhan. The shallowest wells are also located in these lakeshore barangays, indicating the proximity of groundwater to the surface water areas. The study has also identified seven (7) possible cases of unfavorable physical conditions within the immediate well area which have contributed to the contamination of aquifer, and these include (1) submerged well base; (2) well near canal and waterways; (3) cracked well concrete base; (4) absence of a concrete.

Today, City of Santa Rosa has nine (9) water service providers: AJ Mark Realty Corporation, Balibago Waterworks System Inc., Borland Development Corporation, General Trias Water Corporation, Hidalgo Land Development Corporation, Integrated Agro-Industrial Corporation, Laguna AAA Water & Southern Utility Management, Laguna AAA Water Corporation (Laguna Water) and Southern Utility Management and Services Incorporated.

###### SOURCES OF ELECTRICITY

Power Supply in South Luzon Region wherein Santa Rosa City is situated, is generated by power plants operated by the National Power Corporation (NPC) and by Independent Power Producers (IPPs) located in strategic parts of Luzon and supplied to the Luzon Grid attaining a capacity of 9,158 megawatts. Some power plants are located in the region, especially in the Province of Laguna (Makiling Banahaw Geothermal Power Plant), Batangas (Calaca Coal Fired Power Plant) and Quezon (Pagbilao and Hopewell Coal Fired Power Plant). Power is distributed by the Manila Electric Company.

Although the existing structure of MERALCO as the power distributor is deemed equal to the required service provision in the area, it is important that alternative sources of electricity be developed.

Today, almost 100% of the City of Santa Rosa, Laguna households has access to electricity.

The city government initiated the use of solar energy in the city by installing solar power panels in CDRRM Office/Evacuation Center/Command Center located in Barangay Market Area, City of Santa Rosa, Laguna. The city also uses LED in the streetlights. (LED Plan 2021-2025)

###### COMMUNICATION FACILITIES

A good communication service will not only improve the city’s services to its constituents but also increase its economy since all businesses and companies need a reliable communication facility.

At present, the city has six (6) internet service providers: Globe Telecommunication, Philippine Long Distance Telephone (PLDT), Smart Telecommunication, Sky Cable, Royal Cable and Converge ICT; four (4) telephone

service providers: PLDT, Globe Telecommunication, Smart Telecommunication and Dito Telecommunity; six (6) mobile service providers: Smart Communications, Globe Telecom Inc, Digitel Mobile Philippines Inc., Dominer Pointe Inc., and Dito Telecommunity Corporation; seventy-four (74) cell sites and three (3) cable television networks owned by private companies. Postal services are provided by the city postal office and public calling stations are installed inside the city government offices.

There are forty eight (48) local government offices and twenty (20) national government offices situated and operating in the City government of Santa Rosa.

###### Material Recovery Facilities (MRF)

The Materials Recovery Facility (MRF) on each barangay serves as a temporary holding area for the recyclables to be sold in accredited junkshops. The pick-up stations, strategically located in each barangay, are storage area for the biodegradables that will be collected daily by the City ENRO to be processed in the City’s Centralized Composting Facility located in Brgy. Sinalhan. (CSWM Plan, 2016-2025)

The facility has four bioreactors with 1.2MT capacity each. Wastes from the Santa Rosa Public market are also being processed in the facility. These include rotten fruits and vegetables, fruit and vegetable peelings, chicken innards, fish gills, scales and innards. An estimated of 150 kilos of composts per week are being produced from the collected wastes of 300 kilos from the public market. Composts are given for free as per request of the barangays, schools, government employees and farmers through the City Agriculture Office. (CSWM Plan, 20162025)

At present, the City of Santa Rosa has 18 MRFs, one for each barangay, and the location of these MRFs are presented in the table below.

The City of Santa Rosa has completed inventories for both government operations (entity level)1 and LGUgoverned communities (community level)2in response to climate change. Based upon the understanding of the emissions provided by the inventory, the City has culminated a Greenhouse Gas (GHG) Management Framework Plan that sets the LGU’s direction towards low-carbon, climate resilience and sustainability.

Through the Greenhouse Gas Inventory and the Greenhouse Gas Management Framework plan, the city will achieve stabilization of greenhouse gas concentration in the atmosphere at a level that would prevent dangerous anthropogenic interference with the climate system.

The GHG inventory in the City of Santa Rosa shows the overall emissions of the City driven by various sectors. Results of the GHG inventory, which cover the period from January to December 2010 were shown in Table 1.34.

TABLE 1.34. Summary of GHG Inventory Results, City of Santa Rosa, January- December 2010

- 2 Electricity 588,906.59 30.43

- 3 Transportation 119,095.60 6.15

- 4 Solid Waste 103,295.68 5.34

- 5 Wastewater 34,769.02 1.80

- 6 Agriculture 5,494.54 0.28

- 7

Stationary Energy 3,661.53

0.19 100.00

1,935,342.31

Source: City Environment and Natural Resources Office (CENRO)

Results show that 56% (1,080,119.35 tCO2e) of the City’s total GHG emission came from the manufacturing industries, particularly glass, semi-conductor/electronic and air-conditioning. The second largest contributor is the electrical consumption by all sectors (I.e., residential, industrial, commercial, institutional, streetlighting) accounting to 30.43% (588,906.59 tCO2e). Third is the transportation sector at 6.15% (119,095.6 tCO2e). The emissions from solid waste is at fourth, comprising the 5.34% (103,295.68 tCO2e). Other sources include emissions from wastewater, 1.8% (34,769.02 tCO2e); agriculture sector, 0.28% (5,494.54 tCO2e) and stationary energy from residential use, 0.19% (3,661.53 tCO2e). (LCCAP 2016-2025)

1ENTITY LEVEL INVENTORY refers to the inventory of organizational GHG emissions released by activities that are owned by, managed by, or operated on behalf of the Local Government Unit. 2COMMUNITY LEVEL INVENTORY refers to the inventory of community-wide GHG emissions released due to activities occurring within the geopolitical boundary of the Local Government Unit.

The figure above illustrated the results of the GHG Inventory based on the percentage share of the different sources of gas emissions in the city.

Santa Rosa’s water supply is dependent on groundwater. However, recharge area is located outside the LGU (aquifer is in Silang, Cavite). Private water concessionaire (i.e., Laguna Water) also use groundwater as source plus the spring, Matang Tubig, which is located in another local government, City of Cabuyao.

With the increased demand for water as a result of rapid urbanization and intensive land development, the City Government is guarding against possible depletion of groundwater resources in the city and the advent of pollution of the surface water coming from the Silang–Santa Rosa River. Majority of water supply comes from underground sources. The water concessionaire of the City of Santa Rosa, Laguna AAA Water Corporation, sources water from the springs of Matang Tubig in the City of Cabuyao and supplies 10 out of 18 barangays in Santa Rosa. The remaining barangays obtain their water from deep wells, artesian wells, open wells and springs.

The industrialization of Santa Rosa brought negative effects in the waterways (rivers, creeks) that drain to the Laguna Lake. Some companies were reported to be draining their waste into these bodies of water while some companies located in neighboring cities use the Santa Rosa River as outfall for their wastewater. Another contributor is the domestic wastewater due to the absence of a centralized wastewater treatment facility. Presence of informal settlers residing along the riverbanks and lakeshore areas also aggravate the situation. Since there is no centralized domestic wastewater treatment facility prior to discharging to the river system and Laguna Lake outfall, the water quality of these aquatic ecosystems is deteriorating. (LCCAP 2016-2025)

City of Santa Rosa is one-third of the First District of Laguna until August 28, 2019 when it was separated from the City of Biñan and City of San Pedro to be a lone district in Laguna as constituted under the Republic Act No. 11395. The city is politically subdivided into 18 barangays bounded geographically as explained in the city’s Integrated Zoning Ordinance (CLUP 2018-2026).

- Figure 1.28 is a map of the city showing the boundaries among the eighteen barangays. Figure 1.29, on the other hand, shows the barangay hall and its location in the city.

City government of Santa Rosa, as a microcosm of the National Government, has a similar organizational structure as the latter and bounded by the Republic Act No. 7160 also known as the “Local Government Code of 1991”.

The city government is divided into two branches: the Executive branch and the Legislative Branch. From planning to implementation of various programs, projects and activities, these bodies perform their respective mandates to serve the city. With one hundred forty-five (145) Executive Orders, four hundred seventy-six (476) Resolutions and sixty-seven (67) Ordinances from 2020 to 2022, plans and policies are adopted, legalized, institutionalized and implemented.

###### Executive Branch

The Executive branch has the City Mayor or the Local Chief Executive as the presiding officer. Under the Office of the Local Chief Executive are offices with its appointed Department and Unit Heads. There are mandatory offices and there are some that are optional. For the City of Santa Rosa, here is the list of these offices and its current respective Department and Unit heads.

###### Legislative Branch

The Sangguniang Panlungsod is the legislative body of the city government. It is composed of the City ViceMayor as Presiding Officer, twelve (12) regular Sanggunian members or City Councilors, the President of the Association of Barangay Captains, the President of the Sangguniang Kabataan and the Sangguniang Panlungsod Secretary.

This chapter is the main body of the City of Santa Rosa’s Comprehensive Development Plan. It contains all sectoral development plans guided by the vision, mission, goals and objectives of the City of Santa Rosa. The Philippine Development Plan 2023-2028 with President Ferdinand Marcos Jr.’s 8-point agenda, the Sustainable Development Goals, the Ambisyon 2040 and the Regional Development Plans are manifested in the formulation of these sectoral development plans to ensure alignment among plans. From the issues and concerns presented by each sector and supported by the data provided in the local development indicators, a plan of action was developed resulting to programs, projects and activities that will achieve the goals and objectives set by each sector for the improvement and betterment of the city government services to the residents of the City of Santa Rosa.

“Santa Rosa City is Highly Urbanized, Sustainable and Smartest City in the Philippines”

Grounding the vision in practical terms, Santa Rosa City has a mission of becoming a model in local governance effectively responding to the welfare of its people through innovative policies and programs, as well as integrated strategy that is anchored on four (4) important guiding principles as follows:

- ● Creation of a business-friendly and competitive climate;
- ● Support for poverty alleviation and capability building as well as establishment of priority infrastructures;
- ● Protection of environment and promotion of a healthy lifestyle; and
- ● Maintenance of peaceful, orderly and disaster resilient communities.

The vision and mission of the City of Santa Rosa are envisioned to be realized through an integrated and sustainable development strategy, anchored on responsible stewardship, democratic processes and institutions, as well as efficient and effective management that will provide world-class infrastructure and support services to:

- ● Promote the City of Santa Rosa as one of the most friendly business center and the

∙ “Industrial Capital” of South Luzon by facilitating and encouraging local and foreign investments;

- ● Institutionalize community participation in governance;
- ● Provide quality support and social services, e.g. education, health, housing, microentrepreneurship and livelihood programs;

- ● Ensure the protection of its people through rights-based approaches;
- ● Promote and advocate youth and women’s issues and concerns;
- ● Promote local products, heritage sites and eco-tourism;
- ● Promote religious freedom; and,
- ● Promote and protect the environment.

To assess the existing situation of the city and realize its vision, the gap between its current state and ideal state must be identified. A tool used to recognize the difference between these states is the Vision-Reality Gap Analysis (VRGA). VGRA shows how near the current situation in the city to the vision defined by the constituents of the city. It is also used to classify how well a program, projects and activities of the city meets its targeted need. Results of this analysis will guide the sectors in planning programs and projects to be implemented, improved and enhanced.

The table below shows the results of the VRG Analysis and the corresponding action/s to be taken by the City Government to minimize the gap.

The vision-reality gap in 2016 is two hundred (200) points while the current vision-reality gap is one hundred thirty-five (135) points. The thirty-two and a half percent (32.5%) decrease is a result of the city government efforts in addressing the issues and concerns identified in the 2016-2022 Comprehensive Development Plan. Programs and projects delivered to address the highlighted indicators on the above table are among the reasons why the gap was reduced. The COVID-19 pandemic has its share in closing the gap despite of it being the reason for the gap of other indicators to remain unchanged.

This section describes the vision-mission of each sector, the issues and concerns that needs to be addressed and the plan of action to be delivered to achieved the goals and objectives set by each sector. Alignment of these goals and objectives to the Philippine Development Plan(PDP) 2023-2028 and Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) are also presented per sector.

The Comprehensive Development Plan is composed of Sectoral Development Plans prepared and formulated for the city’s efficient and effective governance and are essential to the promotion of the general welfare. These plans contain programs, projects and activities that will ensure and support the preservation and enrichment of culture, promote health and safety, enhance the right of the people to a balanced ecology, encourage and support the development of appropriate and self-reliant scientific and technological capabilities, improve public morals, enhance economic prosperity and social justice, promote full employment among the city’s residents, maintain peace and order, and preserve the comfort and convenience of the city’s inhabitants. (Section 16 of RA 7160)

There are four (4) sectoral development plans in this section. The Social Development Plan with subsectors that focuses on Education, Health, Social Welfare and Social Protection, the Economic Development Plan, the Environmental Development Plan and the Institutional Development Plan. Infrastructure projects are integrated in the sectoral development plans. Also included in each plan are the programs and projects to be implemented with brief descriptions.

![image 645](<853321032-CDP-2023-2028_images/imageFile645.png>)

This chapter comprises the tools needed for the implementation of the Comprehensive Development Plan (CDP) of the City of Santa Rosa. The Local Development Investment Program (LDIP), the main implementation tool for CDP, establishes the connection between the plans and the budget. Implementation period of the LDIP is three years and is annually carried out through the Annual Investment Program (AIP), a year’s worth of PPAs.

In LDIP, sectoral programs and projects are identified based on the thrusts and development agenda of the Local Chief Executive and the City Council. These programs and projects are assessed and then prioritized according to the development requirements of the city and the determination of the level of investible funds. With the list of prioritized PPAs, a financing plan is developed, that duly considers the investible portion of the local budget given its fiscal management capability.

Aside from the budgetary instruments, capability building interventions or programs are formed to address identified capability deficiencies. These intervention programs encompass the Capacity Development Agenda of the local government. Moreover, associated priority legislative requirements are enumerated as integral part in the implementation process of the proposed PPAs in the CDP. These priority legislations are enacted by the Sangguniang Panlungsod to support development priorities of the City government in the medium- and long-term plan.

Lastly, to ensure the efficient, timely and effective implementation of the programs, projects and activities, Annual Accomplishment Report and Monitoring and Evaluation Strategies are outlined and serve as instruments that completes the cyclical nature of planning.

A roster of prioritized programs and projects of the city are provided below. Each program and project are explained in brief with estimated costs The process used in prioritizing these various sectoral programs and projects is also presented.

|PROGRAM|PROJECT 4’S: ANGAT ALIVE FOR ASPIRING ACHIEVEMENTS MADRASAH ECUDATION PROGRAM OF SDO SANTA ROSA CITY<br><br>|
|---|---|
|PROPONENTS|NOEL H. NATIVIDAD, PhD. DPA|
|Brief Description| |
|Arabic Language and Islamic Values Education (ALIVE) under Madrasah Educational Program has been implemented in the Schools Division of Santa Rosa City way back 2004 pursuant to the 1987 Philippine Constitution, and Republic Act (RA) 10533 or the Enhanced Basic Education Act of 2023 that basic education shall be made “learner-oriented and responsive to the needs, cognitive and cultural capacity, the circumstances and diversity of learners, schools, and communities. It shall also recognize the purposes and aspirations of learners for their personal development and participation in national development.<br><br>In accordance with Presidential Decree (PD) 1083, or the Code of Muslim Personal Laws of the Philippines, which provide that “ the State shall consider the customs, traditions, beliefs and interests of national cultural communities in the formulation and implementation of state policies,” and in support of RA 6734, An Act Providing for an Organic act for the Autonomous Region in Muslim Mindanao, which provides the need to establish, maintain and support a complete and integrated system of quality education and adopt an educational framework that is meaningful, relevant and responsive to the needs ideas and spirations of the people in the region,” and of the 1996 Peace Agreement with the Moro national Liberation front, the Department of Education implements the Madrasah Education Program.<br><br>Primarily, the Madrasah Education Program in the Division of Santa Rosa City aims to provide Muslim learners with appropriate and relevant educational opportunities while recognizing their cultural context and unique purposes for participating in the program offerings. It also aims to integrate content and competencies which are relevant and of interest to Muslim learners.<br><br>Balibago Elementary School and Santa Rosa Elementary School Central II, as the main schools in the Division of Santa Rosa City offering Arabic Language and Islamic Values Education under Madrasah Education Program since 2004 experience different challenges in the occurrence of the CIVID 19 Pandemic. In response, DepEd Order No. 25 s 2021 titled: Revised Implementing Guidelines on the Utilization of Fund for Madrasah Education Program necessitates the need for the revision of some provisions in the implementation of Madrasah Education Program in the Division of Santa Rosa City to ensure the continuous delivery education among Muslim Filipino learners in the city.<br><br>In order to continuously improve these programs and services for all learners, SDO Santa Rosa City would like to determine the current status, issues and concerns and identify good practices in the implementation of Arabic Language and Islamic Values Education (ALIVE) Program in schools offering Madrasah Education Program.<br><br>In addition, the program serves as basis in determining an inclusive educational strategy and intervention program for MEP schools that provide the Muslim learners with appropriate and| |

|relevant educational opportunities within the context of their cultures, customs, traditions and interest through the integration of Project 4As (Angat ALIVE for Aspiring Achievements) to make learning authentic and meaningful.| |
|---|---|
|Program Justification| |
|Pursuant to the 1987 Philippine Constitution and Republic Act No. 10533, otherwise known as the Enhanced Basic Education Act of 2013 and DepEd Order No. 25. S. 2021 which aims to provide Muslim learners with appropriate and relevant educational opportunities while recognizing their cultural context and unique purposes for participating in the Program offerings and integrate content and competencies, which are relevant and of interest to Muslim learners.| |
|Intended Beneficiaries| |
|Arabic Language and Islamic Values Education Learners from the two (2) Madrasah Implementing Schools in the Division of Santa Rosa City| |
|Components|Estimated Cost|
|Phase 1 : Management of Curriculum Implementation, Learning Delivery and Technical Assistance (ALIVE Classes)<br><br>1. School Mapping of Learners under ALIVE<br>2. Increase Number of Madrasah Implementing Schools in the Division<br>3. Monitoring of classes and Instructional Supervision in ALIVE Classes<br><br><br>Phase 2 : Curriculum Development, Enrichment and Localization, Learning Recourse and technical Assistance<br><br>1. Planning and program Implementation Review<br>2. Conduct write shop on the enhancement the developed modules based on competencies in Islamic Values and Arabic Language<br>3. Conduct Division Write shop on LEAP (Language Enhancement and Pedagogy)<br><br><br>Phase 3 : Learning Outcomes Assessment and Technical Assistance<br><br>1. School visitations / benchmarking activities for Asatidz<br>2. Division ALIVE Festival of Talents<br><br><br>Phase 4 : Special Curricular Programs and Support Activities<br><br>1. Conduct MUSABAQAH Skills and Competition<br>2. Islamic Values Symposium<br><br><br>|Php. 240,000.00|
| |Timetable|
| |2004 and every year thereafter|

|Target Output/Success Indicators|Possible Risks|
|---|---|
|1. Increase number of ALIVE Learners<br>2. Increase number of Madrasah Education Program Implementing Schools<br>|1. Availability of Funds<br>2. Promotion and Sustainable Support Mechanisms<br>|
|Expected Private Sector Response| |
|Advocacy campaign and resource mobilization support| |

The City Government shall shoulder the tuition fees and miscellaneous fees of the scholars.<br>b.) The program also offers scholarship for the athletes of the city.<br>| | |
